[0:31:21] So there is like this special connection between C and the Unix Linux environments.
[0:31:27] Now, if you're on Mac or something like Ubuntu, all of these commands are going to be exactly the same.
[0:31:33] Now, the actual difference between Unix and Linux, that's not so important for right now.
[0:31:38] All you really need to know is that Linux is a Unix-like operating system.
[0:31:43] So these are actually very similar.
[0:31:45] Because these are so similar, oftentimes people will just use the terms interchangeably.
[0:31:51] That's because if you want to teach these commands that apply to both of these, you don't always want to say Unix slash Linux.
[0:31:57] So if I just say Linux, just assume I'm also talking about Unix, which includes Mac operating systems like OSX.
[0:32:05] So let's get started.

[6:03:57] If it's different in structure, but it serves the same purpose.
[6:04:01] So the purpose of a switch statement is to branch based on certain values.
[6:04:07] And when you use an if statement versus a switch really depends on what you're trying to do.
[6:04:13] Switches are very good for comparing.
[6:04:16] Like let's say we have a simple number like level.
[6:04:19] Like for a video game or for like a gym membership.
[6:04:21] What level are you at?
[6:04:23] And we have different values like 1, 2, or 3, or 4.
[6:04:26] It makes it very easy to branch on those different values.
[6:04:30] If statements allow us to do more complex comparisons in the conditions section of the if statement.
[6:04:38] So if you are trying to do something a little bit more complex, an if statement might be better.
[6:04:44] Personally, I think switch statements are easier to read when we're branching on some very clear variable.
[6:04:50] So with that, when do you use which?
[6:04:53] It's ultimately up to you.
[6:04:54] But after this video, hopefully you have a clear understanding of what a switch statement is.
[6:04:58] And what it does.
[6:05:00] And how to use it.
[6:05:01] Okay, so let's get started.
[6:05:03] So we have a variable int level.
[6:05:06] And its value is 1.
[6:05:08] And what we do is we create a switch statement by just saying switch.
[6:05:11] That's a lower case s.
[6:05:13] And then in parentheses, we put that variable name that we're going to kind of break or branch our program on.
[6:05:21] And then we put the curly braces similar to the body of an if statement.
[6:05:25] But it's a little bit different because inside of these curly braces, what we're going to do is we're going to have different cases.
[6:05:32] And the cases are like the different value possibilities for this variable.
[6:05:37] So for example, we might have case 0.
[6:05:42] And then we put a colon, not a semicolon.
[6:05:46] And then after the case 0, we can put the code of what happens if the value is 0.
[6:05:54] I'm just going to put a comment.
[6:05:57] Code.
[6:05:58] And then at the end of the case, you use what's known as the break keyword.
[6:06:07] And this is a little odd, but you have to put the break keyword.
[6:06:11] And basically that says, hey, I'm done with case 0.
[6:06:15] We can break out of this entire switch statement and proceed to whatever's after the switch statement.
[6:06:22] Then what you do is you can put the next case.
[6:06:25] So you could say case 1.
[6:06:30] And then you put the code and then you have a break.
[6:06:32] So after every case, you need to have this break keyword.
[6:06:38] And it's vitally important because if you don't put this break keyword, the code is going to go through the cases continually, even though you may have already executed one of the cases.
[6:06:49] So the break is important to stop the execution of the different cases.
[6:06:54] And if you don't do it, it's a common mistake and it's called a fall through or something like that.
[6:07:00] Basically, your code is going to fall through all the different cases, even if you don't want it to.
[6:07:05] Once you've got all the cases written out, you're going to have what's known as a default.
[6:07:10] This is very similar to the else statement attached to an if statement.
[6:07:20] And it works the same way.
[6:07:22] And you even actually have to, well, actually, I don't know if it's required or not, but you have to put, in my rules, you have to put break at the end.
[6:07:30] I don't think it's required, but it's a good programming practice because the actual order of these cases doesn't matter.
[6:07:38] So one day you might decide, hey, it would be more clear to me if I put that default case at the top.
[6:07:45] So you might cut and paste that up at the top.
[6:07:48] And if you don't have that break statement, you might forget to add it.
[6:07:51] And it's going to fall through from the default down to the case zero, which you don't want to do.
[6:07:58] So just for best programming practices, every single case, including the default, needs to use that break keyword.
[6:08:07] Some different languages are going to require the break keyword, even if it's at the default at the last.
[6:08:14] So just keep that in mind.
[6:08:16] It's the best thing that you need to do.
[6:08:18] Now, where do you put the code?

[9:31:29] changing the functionality of your program. So this is an entirely behind the scenes thing. So why would
[9:31:35] you want to do this? Why would you want to put effort into changing your code, but keeping your app
[9:31:39] exactly the same? The main, there's a couple of main reasons. The first one is you might have a new way to do the
[9:31:46] code that might be optimized. So for example, you might change an algorithm to a different algorithm
[9:31:51] that has a faster time complexity or a lower time complexity, I guess I should say. Or you might want
[9:31:58] to change code that is sloppy or messy, or you might want to make some new functions, make your code more
[9:32:04] readable. And those are the two primary reasons you would refactor your code. Basically, you're cleaning up
[9:32:09] the code base, you're making it better without changing the functionality of your app. Now, generally,
[9:32:14] what I recommend before you go and refactor a whole project, you will want to have some kind of tests

[9:32:25] application, you're going to have some kind of testing framework. Or even if you don't have that,

[9:38:01] So what the heck is a pointer? So this kind of takes us to new grounds in programming, especially if you
[9:38:10] basically, if you've never programmed, then this is even more crazy. But even if you've used other
[9:38:18] programming languages, they don't really use pointers. So it's new. And because of that, it's
[9:38:25] challenging. So what in the heck is a pointer? It's kind of confusing for beginners. And that's
[9:38:31] okay if you have a hard time. But I'm going to make it very simple for you guys. First think of a normal
[9:38:37] variable. Int x. And this is assigned some value. Now when we do this, inside of our computer,
[9:38:47] there's an area called of memory. There's an area of memory. Another name for memory is RAM. So inside
[9:38:55] of our memory, there is a spot holding the value of x. So if you wanted to think about it that way,
[9:39:02] there's a cell in our memory with the value 5. Now a pointer is a way to point to this location of
[9:39:13] memory. So we can actually basically change the value of this memory or reference it, but not using
[9:39:22] the variable x. We're referencing it indirectly through a pointer. The way you create a pointer
[9:39:28] is you say what type you want to point to. So we're going to point to an int. And then you give it a name.
[9:39:35] So we're going to say pound sign p. You have to use the pound sign. And that is to indicate that it's a
[9:39:46] pointer. Typically that's attached to the name of the variable here. And then what you can say is you
[9:39:52] can set it equal to a value just like we do this. This is an integer variable x. Well here,
[9:40:00] this is an integer pointer p. Then what we do is we can use the address of operator, which we talked
[9:40:08] about in earlier videos. That's going to get the address of a variable in memory x. And what this
[9:40:15] is going to do is it's going to create a variable p. And it's going to point to 5. So it's kind of like
[9:40:25] a variable equal to 5, but not quite. It's the address of the variable equal to 5. So that means
[9:40:34] if we change x to 10, well that means we replaced this value with 10. Thus, the pointer now points to
[9:40:44] 10. Same thing. We can actually change the value through the pointer. So essentially now we have the
[9:40:50] original variable containing the value 10 and we made another variable to point to the same data
[9:40:56] through a pointer pointing to the value 10. So it's kind of like having two variables and they can both
[9:41:03] be used to reference the same data. One directly and one indirectly through a pointer. So pointers can
[9:41:11] be a little bit confusing at first because it's kind of like one of those things where it's hard to
[9:41:15] see the benefit of them until you use them a bunch. And it's kind of like when you're a kid
[9:41:22] and you're starting to learn multiplication. You're like, what am I ever going to do with this in life?
[9:41:28] But as you're older, you start to understand those things. Maybe that was a terrible example. It's just
[9:41:33] something you have to practice and then you're like, oh, I see how this is beneficial. But for now,
[9:41:37] just learn the fundamentals and understand these concepts. And I promise you, it'll be valuable
[9:41:43] later. So the next thing you need to know is what's known as the indirection operator. And it looks
[9:41:49] like the asterisk. So this is confusing. This is what I don't like, but we just have to deal with it.
[9:41:57] When we declared the variable P, we did it like so. And this, this asterisk here says this is a pointer.
[9:42:06] This is different than the asterisk here, which is the indirection operator. I hate that,
[9:42:13] but we just got to deal with it. So when we declare the variable, we use an asterisk. Then if we want
[9:42:20] to get the value that that variable was pointing to, so P points to the value five, if we want to get
[9:42:29] that value, we use the indirection operator before the variable P. So what that means, since P was
[9:42:38] originally pointing to X, what we could do is we could say X equals 10 and change that value to 10.
[9:42:45] Or what we could do is we could say the value at P is equal to 20. And that'll change the value.
[9:42:55] At this point, if you printed X, it would print 20. So the benefit here is that once we understand
[9:43:04] pointers, we can use pointers to functions. And this will allow functions to change the values of
[9:43:11] variables. And that can be really valuable if you need to do something like a swap function. You're not
[9:43:18] able to change the value of variables unless you're using pointers. So definitely useful,
[9:43:24] definitely important. You just got to wrap your brain around some different concepts. The three
[9:43:28] things you really need to understand is how you declare them like this, how you assign to them,
[9:43:36] which is like this, P equals address of some variable X. And then, so that's the second way,
[9:43:43] that's the second thing you need to know. And then how you reference the value where the pointer points
[9:43:47] to, which is with the indirection operator. So this can also be called dereferencing. So if you
[9:43:54] dereference a pointer, you're basically getting the value where that pointer is pointing to.
